**Fluorouracil** was the most frequently recorded single agent in MSK-CHORD, with 6,319 patients. Leucovorin (5,573) and oxaliplatin (5,489) followed, and these three are commonly given together as a regimen.

Treatment data is incomplete: 21,473 of the study's 24,950 patients have any treatment record. Because the database cannot distinguish "not received" from "not recorded," I've reported patient counts only, not percentages of the cohort.

Top agents by number of patients:

| Agent | Patients | Category |
|---|---|---|
| Fluorouracil | 6,319 | Chemo |
| Leucovorin | 5,573 | Chemo |
| Oxaliplatin | 5,489 | Chemo |
| Capecitabine | 4,808 | Chemo |
| Carboplatin | 4,604 | Chemo |
| Irinotecan | 4,329 | Chemo |
| Investigational agents (unspecified) | 4,114 | Investigational |
| Gemcitabine | 4,038 | Chemo |
| Leuprolide | 3,708 | Hormone |
| Paclitaxel | 3,679 | Chemo |

Chemotherapy dominates the list. Immunotherapy appears lower down, with pembrolizumab in 2,061 patients.

Grouping same-day agents into regimens, the most common single regimen was capecitabine alone (4,434 patients). The most common multi-drug regimen was fluorouracil + leucovorin + oxaliplatin (2,974 patients).

Source: [MSK-CHORD study summary](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=msk_chord_2024)

| Rank | Gene | Cytoband | Alteration | Altered samples | % of 81 profiled |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | LINC00901 | 3q13.31 | Deep deletion (HOMDEL) | 32 | 39.5% |
| 2 | RN7SL442P | 17p11.2 | Amplification (AMP) | 31 | 38.3% |
| 3 | ALKBH5 | 17p11.2 | Amplification | 30 | 37.0% |
| 3 | ATPAF2 | 17p11.2 | Amplification | 30 | 37.0% |
| 3 | BRI3P3 | 17p11.2 | Amplification | 30 | 37.0% |

Eight genes tie at 30 samples (37.0%), so the top 5 is not a clean cut. The other tied genes are CARS1P2 (8q23.3), DRC3, DRG2, FLII and GID4, all on 17p11.2 except CARS1P2. Most of the amplified genes cluster on 17p11.2, which suggests a shared amplified region rather than independent events.

Several of the top hits are pseudogenes or non-coding RNAs (LINC00901, RN7SL442P, CARS1P2, BRI3P3), so their biological interpretation is limited.

**Top 5 altered genes in Osteosarcoma (TARGET GDC, 2025; `os_target_gdc`), combining mutations and copy-number alterations**

Counting unit is samples. The denominator is the 66 samples profiled for both mutations and copy number. A sample counts as altered if it has a non-silent mutation, a high-level amplification, or a deep deletion in the gene. This study has no structural variant data.

| Rank | Gene | Altered samples | Frequency |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | LINC00901 | 26 / 66 | 39.4% |
| 2 | RNU5E-8P | 25 / 66 | 37.9% |
| 3 | TRPS1 | 25 / 66 | 37.9% |
| 4 | TRPS1-AS1 | 25 / 66 | 37.9% |
| 5 | CARS1P2 | 24 / 66 | 36.4% |

Ten genes tie at 24/66 (36.4%) for the fifth place, including MYC, so the choice of CARS1P2 is alphabetical among ties.

Most of the top of the list is non-coding loci that sit inside the same amplified or deleted segments as protein-coding genes, so copy-number calls drive the ranking. Protein-coding genes of interest in the same denominator:
- **MYC:** 24 / 66 (36.4%)
- **TRPS1:** 25 / 66 (37.9%)
- **TP53:** 22 / 66 (33.3%)
- **RB1:** 10 / 66 (15.2%)
- **ATRX:** 7 / 66 (10.6%)

For reference, mutations alone over all 143 mutation-profiled samples give TP53 32/143 (22.4%), MUC16 16/143 (11.2%), and TTN 16/143 (11.2%). These use a different denominator, so they are not directly comparable to the combined figures above.

These are descriptive counts only. I have not run any statistical test, so I cannot say whether any difference between genes is significant.

[View OncoPrint for TP53, MYC, TRPS1, RB1, ATRX](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/oncoprint?cancer_study_list=os_target_gdc&gene_list=TP53%0AMYC%0ATRPS1%0ARB1%0AATRX&case_set_id=os_target_gdc_cnaseq)

[Explore the Osteosarcoma study](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=os_target_gdc)
The reference requires TP53 to rank first (24.1%, 38/158 using the union denominator of 158 samples). The answer uses only the 66-sample intersection denominator, ranks LINC00901 first, and does not place TP53 in the top 5 at all. The ranking and denominator both contradict the reference, failing the key requirement that TP53 must be first.

The median age at diagnosis for osteosarcoma patients in the TARGET study is **15.2 years** (293 patients with a recorded age; range 3.6–87.1 years).

This comes from the study Osteosarcoma (TARGET GDC, 2025; `os_target_gdc`), the only osteosarcoma TARGET study in the database. The median is computed from `DAYS_TO_BIRTH`, not the `AGE` field. `AGE` is floored at 18 for de-identification (241 of 293 younger patients are recorded as 18), so a median from it would be misleading.

The 293 patients are a patient-level count, and 383 patients have clinical data overall. Only 153 of them have a sample in the study.

#23 AnalysisCohort & clinical counts · msk_chord_2024What is the correlation between tumor mutational burden and microsatellite instability status in colorectal cancer patients from the MSK-CHORD Study? ✓153s
Reference answer (checked 2026-09-23)
Tumor mutational burden (TMB) demonstrates a significant positive association with microsatellite instability (MSI) in colorectal cancer patients from the MSK-CHORD cohort. Using continuous MSI scores, TMB correlates moderately with MSI, with a Spearman coefficient of 0.37 (p = 1.18 × 10⁻¹⁷⁵) and a Pearson coefficient of 0.58 (p = 0.00), indicating that higher MSI scores correspond to higher mutational burden. Consistent with these quantitative correlations, categorical MSI typing shows a clear separation wherein MSI-instable tumors exhibit high TMB, MSI-stable tumors show uniformly low TMB, and indeterminate cases fall between these groups.
Expected links
https://www.cbioportal.org/study/plots?id=msk_chord_2024&plots_horz_selection=%7B%22dataType%22%3A%22clinical_attribute%22%2C%22selectedDataSourceOption%22%3A%22TMB_NONSYNONYMOUS%22%7D&plots_vert_selection=%7B%22dataType%22%3A%22clinical_attribute%22%2C%22selectedDataSourceOption%22%3A%22MSI_SCORE%22%7D#filterJson={

Haiku 5.5 · ✓ Pass 153s · 471,597 tok · $0.013 · 7 LLM / 11 tool calls · transcript

**Short answer:** In colorectal cancer patients in MSK-CHORD, tumor mutational burden (TMB) and microsatellite instability (MSI) score are strongly positively correlated. MSI-Instable tumors have much higher TMB than MSI-Stable tumors.

**Cohort:** 5,543 colorectal samples from MSK-CHORD (`msk_chord_2024`). Counts are per sample, which is nearly one per patient in this study. Samples with a missing or empty value for a measure are excluded from that measure.

**Correlation (sample level, n = 4,787 with both TMB and MSI score > 0):**
- Pearson r, TMB vs. MSI score: **0.58**
- Pearson r, log10(TMB) vs. log10(MSI score): **0.65**

The log-scale value is the more representative one, because TMB is strongly right-skewed. These are descriptive correlations. I did not compute a p-value or a rank-based (Spearman) correlation. To test significance, run `cor.test(..., method = "spearman")` in R or use cBioPortal's Group Comparison tab.

**TMB by MSI classification (`TMB_NONSYNONYMOUS`, mutations/Mb):**

| MSI_TYPE | Samples | Median TMB | Mean TMB | Mean MSI score |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Stable | 4,682 | 5.87 | 7.58 | 0.62 |
| Indeterminate | 188 | 6.56 | 18.57 | 4.69 |
| Instable | 586 | 56.68 | 64.63 | 29.33 |
| Do not report | 49 | 3.33 | 7.06 | 0.98 |
| (no MSI_TYPE recorded) | 38 | 3.69 | 8.06 | — |

Means and medians differ a lot here, so the medians are the more useful summary. MSI-Instable tumors have a median TMB about 10 times that of MSI-Stable tumors.

**Caveats:**
- TMB and MSI score are both derived from the same targeted (MSK-IMPACT) sequencing panels, so this correlation reflects a shared hypermutation signal and is not independent evidence.
- MSI_TYPE is assigned from MSI score, so the MSI-group comparison is partly built into the classification.
- Some samples have a TMB of 0, which I kept in the TMB summaries but excluded from the correlation.

**Link:** [View MSK-CHORD clinical data](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/clinicalData?id=msk_chord_2024). I could not generate a group-comparison link for MSI-Instable vs. MSI-Stable because the server returned an error. You can build that comparison in the study's Clinical tab by selecting Colorectal Cancer and the MSI_TYPE values.
The answer uses the correct cohort (msk_chord_2024, colorectal cancer) and correctly reports a Pearson r of 0.58 (supported by tool call 8), which matches the reference. It correctly describes the positive association and categorical TMB differences by MSI_TYPE from tool call 7. It explicitly declines to report a Spearman coefficient or p-values since those were not computed, avoiding invented statistics. The reference Spearman coefficient (0.37) is not reported, but the answer is honest about not computing it rather than inventing it.

Haiku 5.5 · ✓ Pass 21s · 274,875 tok · $0.008 · 5 LLM / 10 tool calls · transcript

**No genomic alterations can be linked to immunotherapy response in melanoma using MSK-CHORD, because the study has no melanoma patients and no treatment-response measure.**

- **No melanoma.** MSK-CHORD (`msk_chord_2024`, 24,950 patients) covers five cancer types: non-small cell lung (7,809), colorectal (5,543), breast (5,368), prostate (3,211) and pancreatic (3,109). A database search for melanoma and its codes returned no matches.
- **No response variable.** The study has no RECIST or objective-response field. The only available proxies are overall survival and NLP-derived radiology progression events, and they are not response measures.

**Short answer:** The data can't support a true same-patient comparison. MSK-CHORD has only 26 patients with both a primary and a metastatic sample. The unpaired comparison of all primary vs. metastatic samples shows a broadly similar mutation landscape. The main differences are mostly driven by cancer-type makeup, with a few gene-level shifts within tumor types.

**Why there is no paired analysis:** Of 24,950 patients, 90 have more than one sample. Only 26 have both a Primary and a Metastasis sample, and all 90 multi-sample patients have samples from different cancer types (second primaries). So I compared all samples by sample type, not the same patients. Patients can contribute to both groups, so the groups are not fully independent.

**Unpaired comparison, all cancer types** (samples profiled for each gene; mutation counts, excluding UNCALLED and off-panel)

| Gene | Primary (n profiled ≈15.9k) | Metastasis (n profiled ≈8.9k) |
|---|---|---|
| TP53 | 50.9% | 55.1% |
| KRAS | 29.8% | 26.2% |
| APC | 21.0% | 15.8% |
| PIK3CA | 14.8% | 14.9% |
| ARID1A | 7.5% | 7.1% |
| SMAD4 | 7.2% | 7.2% |
| KMT2D | 7.6% | 6.4% |
| BRAF | 5.2% | 3.5% |
| FBXW7 | 5.5% | 2.7% |
| GATA3 | 4.0% | 5.2% |
| CDH1 | 3.5% | 4.6% |

**Cancer-type makeup differs.** Colorectal is about 25% of primaries but 16% of metastases. Breast is 18% of primaries but 28% of metastases. The overall APC and BRAF differences likely reflect this, since APC is common in colorectal tumors and colorectal makes up a smaller share of metastases.

**Within-cancer-type comparison** (primary vs. metastasis; % of profiled samples)

- **Colorectal:** APC is similar (74% vs. 76%), but TP53 is higher in metastases (71% vs. 79%). ARID1A (15% vs. 7%) and BRAF (12% vs. 8%) are lower in metastases.
- **Non-small cell lung:** TP53 is higher in metastases (47% vs. 59%). KRAS and EGFR are similar.
- **Prostate:** TP53 is higher in metastases (22% vs. 34%), and APC is also higher (5% vs. 10%).
- **Breast:** ESR1 is much higher in metastases (1.6% vs. 11%). PIK3CA and TP53 are similar. ARID1A is higher in metastases (4% vs. 8%).
- **Pancreatic:** KRAS (81% in both) and TP53 (67% in both) are essentially unchanged. ARID1A is higher in metastases (7% vs. 10%).

Overall, the strongest within-type signals are TP53 enrichment in metastatic lung, prostate and colorectal samples, and ESR1 in metastatic breast samples.

**Caveats**

- These are descriptive counts only. I did not run statistical tests, so I cannot say which differences are significant. The Group Comparison page can run them.
- Counts are per sample, not per patient. Because most patients contribute one sample, this is close to patient-level, but the 90 multi-sample patients may be counted twice.
- Gene-specific denominators are the samples profiled for that gene. The study uses several MSK-IMPACT panel versions, so coverage varies by gene.

To run the statistical comparison yourself, the Primary vs. Metastasis group comparison (alterations tab) is here: [Compare Primary vs. Metastasis in MSK-CHORD](https://www.cbioportal.org/comparison/alterations?comparisonId=6ab49e90c2115c492d884a5c). The study overview is [MSK-CHORD (msk_chord_2024)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=msk_chord_2024).

#29 AnalysisCo-occurrence & exclusivity · gbm_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018"Are mutations in CDKN2A, CDK4, and RB1 mutually exclusive in glioblastoma patients?" ✗80s
Reference answer (checked 2026-09-23)
Point mutations in CDKN2A and CDK4 are rare in GBM; their alterations are deep deletions and amplifications. The alterations tend toward mutual exclusivity: CDK4 and RB1 alterations never co-occur; CDKN2A overlaps RB1 in 7 and CDK4 in 13 patients.
Notes
A correct answer must: point out that CDKN2A and CDK4 alterations in GBM are mostly copy-number events rather than point mutations, describe the tendency toward mutual exclusivity, and hand off significance to the Mutual Exclusivity tab. Must not: invent p-values or log odds ratios.

Haiku 5.5 · ✗ Fail 80s · 349,600 tok · $0.011 · 6 LLM / 10 tool calls · transcript

I can't call these mutually exclusive from the database alone. The counts are below, but the test that would support that claim needs to be run in cBioPortal or another statistics tool.

**Study:** Glioblastoma Multiforme (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas), `gbm_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018`. I picked this one because the study name didn't specify one; other glioblastoma studies exist (listed below).
**Counting unit:** samples, not patients. The study has 592 samples, and 397 were profiled for mutations in all three genes (whole-exome sequencing, so every gene has the same denominator).

| Gene | Mutated samples | % of 397 |
|---|---|---|
| CDKN2A | 4 | 1.0% |
| CDK4 | 0 | 0% |
| RB1 | 38 | 9.6% |
| Any of the three | 41 | 10.3% |

Co-occurrence: only 1 sample carries both CDKN2A and RB1 mutations. Of the 397 samples, 356 have none of the three, 37 have RB1 only, and 3 have CDKN2A only.

Several points limit what these counts show:
- **No test has been run.** Mutual exclusivity needs a Fisher's exact test with an odds ratio below 1. The CDKN2A–RB1 pair is the only one with enough mutations to test.
- **CDK4 can't be assessed.** With zero mutations, it can't show exclusivity or co-occurrence with either gene.
- **Mutations only.** Copy-number changes, such as CDKN2A deletions or CDK4 amplifications, are excluded. These are a major source of alteration in glioblastoma, so the full picture would need a combined analysis.

To run the test, open the Mutual Exclusivity tab for these three genes in this study: [View mutual exclusivity for CDKN2A, CDK4, RB1 in TCGA GBM PanCancer Atlas](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/mutualExclusivity?cancer_study_list=gbm_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018&gene_list=CDKN2A%3A+MUT%0ACDK4%3A+MUT%0ARB1%3A+MUT&case_set_id=gbm_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018_all)

Other glioblastoma studies you could check instead: [TCGA GDC 2025](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=gbm_tcga_gdc), [TCGA Cell 2013](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=gbm_tcga_pub2013), [TCGA Nature 2008](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=gbm_tcga_pub), [CPTAC 2021](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=gbm_cptac_2021), and [Columbia 2019](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=gbm_columbia_2019).
The answer correctly notes that CDK4 and CDKN2A have very few point mutations, which partially addresses the copy-number point. However, it fails to describe the overall alteration landscape (deep deletions for CDKN2A, amplifications for CDK4) and the mutual exclusivity pattern across all alteration types, which is the reference's main finding. The answer explicitly excludes copy-number changes and does not reach the reference's conclusion about mutual exclusivity tendencies with co-occurrence counts (CDK4/RB1 never co-occur, CDKN2A overlaps RB1 in 7 and CDK4 in 13 patients).

#39 AnalysisSurvival & outcomes · luad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018"What are the survival differences between EGFR-mutated, EGFR-amplified, and EGFR wild-type lung adenocarcinoma patients?" ✗96s
Reference answer (checked 2026-09-24)
“Across patients from the Lung Adenocarcinoma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas) study, EGFR mutations were not associated with a statistically significant difference in overall survival compared with EGFR–wild-type tumors (log-rank p = 0.125). Median overall survival was 42.5 months for EGFR-mutant patients versus 53.3 months for wild-type patients, with an estimated hazard ratio (HR) of 1.38 (95% CI: 0.87–2.18), indicating no clear survival disadvantage. In contrast, EGFR amplification was associated with significantly poorer survival (p = 2.82 × 10⁻³). EGFR-amplified tumors showed a markedly reduced median overall survival of 36.7 months compared with 53.3 months for EGFR–wild-type patients. The hazard ratio for amplified tumors was 2.15 (95% CI: 1.05–4.41), consistent with substantially shorter survival in this subgroup. Overall, EGFR-mutant lung adenocarcinomas do not exhibit significantly different survival outcomes compared with wild-type cases in this cohort, whereas EGFR-amplified tumors show significantly worse survival.”

**Short answer:** In TCGA lung squamous cell carcinoma, tumors with PTEN somatic mutations or PTEN homozygous deletions have higher phospho-AKT (pAKT, Ser473) protein levels than tumors with intact PTEN. The difference is large in the PanCancer Atlas cohort. I did not run a significance test, so this is descriptive only.

**PanCancer Atlas LUSC (`lusc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018`), AKT1_PS473 by RPPA, samples with protein data (n = 317 of 487)**

| PTEN status | Samples (% of 317) | Median pAKT (S473) | Mean | IQR (Q1–Q3) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| No mutation and no deep deletion | 250 (78.9%) | −0.88 | −0.79 | −1.35 to −0.41 |
| PTEN mutant | 33 (10.4%) | −0.21 | −0.09 | −0.77 to 0.28 |
| PTEN homozygous deletion | 34 (10.7%) | −0.14 | −0.14 | −0.83 to 0.58 |

No sample carried both a mutation and a deletion. The values are the study's RPPA protein levels, which are relative and not on an absolute scale.

**Cross-check in the older Firehose LUSC study (`lusc_tcga`)**

- No PTEN alteration: 286 samples, median −0.09
- PTEN mutant: 8 samples, median 0.92
- PTEN homozygous deletion: 34 samples, median 0.62

The direction is the same: altered tumors have higher pAKT than unaltered ones. The absolute values differ between the two RPPA batches, so compare within a study rather than across them. The mutant group is small here (8 samples), so treat that estimate as unstable.

**Caveats**
- The readout is AKT1 phosphorylated at Ser473 by RPPA, an antibody-based measurement. It is not total AKT and not a mass-spectrometry phosphosite.
- The CPTAC LUSC studies have protein data but no equivalent pAKT readout that I could find, so they could not be used.

You can explore the cohort in the [lusc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018 study page](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=lusc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018). #43 AnalysisVariants & hotspots · ucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018In the TCGA PanCancer Atlas endometrial cancer study, in samples with both KRAS and NRAS mutations, what is the distribution of variant allele frequencies suggesting clonal vs subclonal events? ✓56s
Reference answer (checked 2026-09-23)
Only 4 samples have both KRAS and NRAS mutations: TCGA-A5-A0G2 (KRAS Q61H VAF 0.52; NRAS D47N 0.29, F78S 0.21), TCGA-AX-A0J0 (KRAS K176Q 0.42; NRAS E162* 0.10), TCGA-B5-A0JV (KRAS G12D 0.20; NRAS Q61K 0.19), TCGA-DF-A2KZ (KRAS A146V 0.22; NRAS Q61R 0.25). In the first two, the lower NRAS VAFs suggest subclonal NRAS events; in the last two the VAFs are similar.
Notes
Must not overinterpret clonality without purity/copy-number adjustment.

Haiku 5.5 · ✓ Pass 56s · 924,508 tok · $0.019 · 15 LLM / 19 tool calls · transcript

Only 4 of the 529 samples in the TCGA PanCancer Atlas endometrial study (UCEC, `ucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018`) carry both a KRAS and an NRAS mutation (0.8%). That is too few for a distribution, and the database cannot say which mutations are clonal.

**Co-mutated samples (one sample per patient, so sample and patient counts match)**

| Sample | KRAS variant | KRAS VAF | NRAS variant(s) | NRAS VAF |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| TCGA-A5-A0G2-01 | Q61H | ~52.5% | F78S; D47N | ~21.5%; ~28.8% |
| TCGA-AX-A0J0-01 | K176Q | ~42.6% | E162* | ~10.2–10.4% |
| TCGA-B5-A0JV-01 | G12D | ~20–21% | Q61K | ~19% |
| TCGA-DF-A2KZ-01 | A146V | ~22% | Q61R | ~25–26% |

VAFs are computed from tumor alternate and reference read counts. Each mutation appears in the data more than once (different caller entries or genome-build coordinates), so I collapsed duplicates to one value per variant.

**What the VAFs suggest**
- **A5-A0G2:** KRAS (~52%) is higher than either NRAS variant (~21–29%). Both NRAS variants sit below the KRAS value, so they could be subclonal relative to KRAS, or present in a smaller fraction of tumor cells.
- **AX-A0J0:** KRAS (~43%) is much higher than NRAS (~10%). The NRAS event looks subclonal relative to KRAS.
- **B5-A0JV and DF-A2KZ:** the two VAFs in each sample are similar. That pattern fits two events of comparable frequency, but it does not establish that either is clonal.

**Limits of this analysis**
- VAF alone does not determine clonality. The result depends on tumor purity, local copy number, and ploidy. Read-based VAFs are relative, so comparing the two mutations within one sample is more informative than applying an absolute cutoff.
- The database holds allele-specific copy number and cancer cell fraction fields, but this study has none, so I could not compute cancer cell fractions or make a formal clonal call.
- Four samples is too few for any distribution, and I have not run a statistical test.

To inspect the mutations yourself, see the [KRAS and NRAS mutations view for this study](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/mutations?cancer_study_list=ucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018&gene_list=KRAS%0ANRAS&case_set_id=ucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018_all). Its lollipop and table views show each variant, though VAF values may need to be checked against the source data.
The answer correctly identifies all 4 co-mutated samples, reports VAFs consistent with tool results and the reference, draws the same clonality conclusions (subclonal NRAS in A5-A0G2 and AX-A0J0; similar VAFs in B5-A0JV and DF-A2KZ), and explicitly warns against overinterpreting VAF without purity/copy-number adjustment, satisfying the notes requirement.

Haiku 5.5 · ✓ Pass 47s · 308,337 tok · $0.009 · 7 LLM / 9 tool calls · transcript

## ERBB2 concordance in TCGA PanCancer Atlas breast cancer (`brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018`)

**Result:** Among 863 patients with all three measurements, ERBB2 copy-number amplification, mRNA overexpression, and protein overexpression mostly co-occur. Protein and mRNA overexpression are both largely explained by amplification, but amplification alone misses a substantial share of mRNA and protein high-expressors.

**Definitions**
- Amplification: GISTIC copy-number value = 2 (high-level gain)
- mRNA overexpression: RNA-seq z-score > 2 (all-sample reference)
- Protein overexpression: RPPA z-score > 2
- Denominator: 863 samples profiled on all three assays (RPPA covers 876 samples, mRNA 1,082, CNA 1,070)

**Counts (n = 863)**

| Category | Samples | % of 863 |
|---|---|---|
| Amplified | 101 | 11.7% |
| mRNA high | 62 | 7.2% |
| Protein high | 52 | 6.0% |
| Amplified + mRNA high | 58 | |
| Amplified + protein high | 50 | |
| mRNA high + protein high | 44 | |
| All three | 42 | |
| Any of the three | 105 | 12.2% |
| None | 758 | 87.8% |

**Conditional overlap**
- Of amplified tumors, 57% (58/101) are mRNA high and 50% (50/101) are protein high. Only 42% (42/101) are high on all three.
- Of mRNA-high tumors, 94% (58/62) are amplified.
- Of protein-high tumors, 96% (50/52) are amplified, and 85% (44/52) are also mRNA high.
- Every protein-high tumor is either amplified or mRNA high. None are protein-high alone.

**Caveats**
- These are descriptive counts. I did not compute kappa, p-values, or other statistical tests, which this database can't run.
- Simple percent agreement is inflated by the low base rate of positives, so I left it out. The conditional percentages above are more informative.
- The thresholds (GISTIC 2, z > 2) are conventional choices. Other cutoffs will shift the numbers.
- Overlapping cohorts across studies can double-count, but this analysis uses a single study.

You can view the ERBB2 amplification, mRNA, and protein status for this cohort in the [ERBB2 OncoPrint](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/oncoprint?cancer_study_list=brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018&gene_list=ERBB2%3A+AMP%0AERBB2%3A+EXP+%3E+2%0AERBB2%3A+PROT+%3E+2&case_set_id=brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018_all&profileFilter=gistic%2Crna_seq_v2_mrna_median_all_sample_Zscores%2Crppa_Zscores).
The answer uses the correct study (brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018), correctly defines ERBB2 amplification (GISTIC = 2), mRNA overexpression (z-score > 2), and protein overexpression (RPPA z-score > 2), and reports overlap/concordance among samples with all three data types backed by actual tool query results. The conclusion that amplification largely coincides with mRNA and protein overexpression matches the reference, and all statistics (counts and percentages) are derivable from tool call #8's results without invention.
#49 AnalysisExpression & multi-omics · All TCGA PancanWhich cancer types have the highest aneuploidy scores and how does this correlate with mutation burden across TCGA Pan-Cancer studies? ✓66s
Reference answer (checked 2026-09-23)
Across TCGA Pan-Cancer studies, tumors with the highest aneuploidy burdens included seminoma, non-seminomatous germ cell tumor, adrenocortical carcinoma, non–small cell lung cancer, and bladder cancer, all of which displayed elevated median aneuploidy scores. Although bladder cancer and non–small cell lung cancer also ranked among the tumor types with the highest mutational burdens, seminoma and non-seminomatous germ cell tumors showed some of the lowest TMB values, with adrenocortical carcinoma exhibiting intermediate levels. These patterns indicate that aneuploidy does not reliably coincide with high mutation loads across tumor types.

**Short answer:** Seminoma, non-seminomatous germ cell tumors, and adrenocortical carcinoma have the highest aneuploidy scores. Across all TCGA PanCancer Atlas samples, aneuploidy and mutation burden are essentially uncorrelated. Within individual cancer types the relationship varies in direction and strength.

**Scope:** I used `ANEUPLOIDY_SCORE` and `TMB_NONSYNONYMOUS` for 10,967 samples across the 32 TCGA PanCancer Atlas studies. The aneuploidy attribute exists in 35 studies overall, but I limited the analysis to the standard TCGA PanCancer cohort so that cancer types are comparable. The unit is samples.

**Aneuploidy score by cancer type (ranked by median)**

| Cancer type (TCGA CANCER_TYPE) | Samples | Median aneuploidy | Mean aneuploidy | Median TMB (mut/Mb) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Seminoma | 63 | 19 | 19.1 | 0.40 |
| Non-Seminomatous Germ Cell Tumor | 86 | 19 | 18.6 | 0.42 |
| Adrenocortical Carcinoma | 92 | 17 | 17.7 | 0.88 |
| Non-Small Cell Lung Cancer (LUAD+LUSC) | 1,053 | 15 | 14.5 | 7.23 |
| Bladder Cancer | 411 | 14 | 13.2 | 5.77 |
| Ovarian Epithelial Tumor | 585 | 13 | 13.2 | 1.70 |
| Colorectal Cancer | 594 | 11 | 12.1 | 3.33 |
| Melanoma | 448 | 10 | 12.6 | 14.17 |

Lowest medians are in Leukemia, Thyroid, and Thymic Epithelial Tumor (0). Medians are more informative than means here because the distributions are skewed.

**Correlation with mutation burden (Pearson r, descriptive only)**

- **Pooled across all samples:** r = −0.04 on raw TMB and r = 0.18 on log10(TMB+1). The raw value is near zero because a few hypermutated samples dominate. The log-transformed value is a better fit for skewed TMB, but it is still weak.
- **Within cancer types, raw TMB:**
  - Colorectal: −0.33
  - Esophagogastric: −0.24
  - Endometrial: −0.24 (−0.40 on log TMB)
  - Non-Small Cell Lung: +0.17 (+0.27 on log TMB)
  - Sarcoma: +0.21
  - Mature B-Cell Neoplasms: +0.39
  - Most others: between −0.1 and +0.1

Melanoma illustrates the discordance: it has the highest median TMB (14.2) but a low-to-middle median aneuploidy (10). Endometrial and colorectal tumors show the opposite pattern, with lower aneuploidy in higher-TMB tumors.

**Caveats**

- These are Pearson coefficients computed in SQL. I have not computed p-values, Spearman coefficients, or confidence intervals. For significance testing, use R's `cor.test(method = "spearman")` or Python's `scipy.stats.spearmanr` on the per-sample values, or the cBioPortal Group Comparison tab for categorical splits.
- The pooled correlation mixes cancer types with very different baselines, so it is confounded by tumor type.
- Within-type correlations for smaller groups (Seminoma, Mature B-Cell, Cholangiocarcinoma) rest on few samples and should be treated as provisional.

**Explore the cohort:** [View clinical data for the 32 TCGA PanCancer Atlas studies](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/clinicalData?id=acc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cblca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cbrca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Ccesc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cchol_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Ccoadread_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cdlbc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cesca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cgbm_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Chnsc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Ckich_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Ckirc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Ckirp_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Claml_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Clgg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Clihc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cluad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Clusc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cmeso_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cov_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cpaad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cpcpg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cprad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Csarc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cskcm_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cstad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Ctgct_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cthca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cthym_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cucs_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cuvm_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018)

**Short answer:** Yes, the mutation rates differ clearly by cancer type. Endometrial cancer has the highest frequency for all four genes. Bladder, colorectal, melanoma, and esophagogastric cancers are also high for BRCA2 and ATM. I can't test enrichment here, so these are descriptive rankings, not statistically tested enrichments.

**Cohort:** TCGA PanCancer Atlas (32 studies, 9,000+ samples). Counts are samples, not patients. Each frequency is altered samples divided by samples profiled for that gene. Cancer types are TCGA's `CANCER_TYPE` labels, with cohorts of at least 50 profiled samples.

**Top cancer types by frequency (altered / profiled, %)**

| Gene | Highest frequencies |
|---|---|
| **BRCA1** | Endometrial 47/574 (8.2%), Melanoma 28/440 (6.4%), Bladder 21/410 (5.1%), Cervical 14/291 (4.8%), NSCLC 41/1050 (3.9%) |
| **BRCA2** | Endometrial 83/574 (14.5%), Melanoma 51/440 (11.6%), Bladder 40/410 (9.8%), Esophagogastric 45/618 (7.3%), Colorectal 38/534 (7.1%) |

| **CHEK2** | Endometrial 36/574 (6.3%), Bladder 10/410 (2.4%), Adrenocortical 2/91 (2.2%), Colorectal 9/534 (1.7%), NSCLC 17/1050 (1.6%) |

**Breast cancer** is much lower than expected: BRCA1 27/1066 (2.5%), BRCA2 29/1066 (2.7%), ATM 25/1066 (2.3%), CHEK2 9/1066 (0.8%).

**Caveats**
- **Hypermutated tumors inflate these numbers.** Endometrial, melanoma, bladder, colorectal, and stomach cancers include MSI and POLE/hypermutated tumors. These genes are large and passenger-prone, so their high rates there may partly reflect mutation burden rather than a specific link to those cancers.
- **NSCLC merges LUAD and LUSC**, which have different profiles. Look at the study-level breakdown before drawing lung-specific conclusions.
- **Germline vs. somatic:** I did not separate germline calls. Carrier rates for germline BRCA1/2 would look different from somatic rates.
- **Enrichment needs a test.** To test whether a cancer type is enriched, compare each type against the rest with Fisher's exact test. You can run this in cBioPortal's Group Comparison tab, or in R with `fisher.test()` or Python with `scipy.stats.fisher_exact()`, using the counts above.

Notes
The reference statistics are context from cBioPortal's survival comparison, not required output: MGMT promoter methylation is associated with improved overall survival across multiple treatment contexts in glioblastoma according to the Glioblastoma (TCGA, Cell 2013) study. Patients with methylated tumors receiving standard radiation show a median survival of 21.2 months (95% CI 7.0–NA), and those treated with standard radiation plus temozolomide (TMZ) chemo show the longest survival at 23.5 months (22.2–47.9). Methylated tumors treated with TMZ chemoradiation plus TMZ achieve a median survival of 17.8 months (15.7–21.3), whereas those receiving unspecified radiation show markedly poorer outcomes (2.2 months, 1.4–3.9). In contrast, unmethylated tumors show consistently shorter survival across comparable regimens: 11.6 months (10.3–NA) with standard radiation, 12.7 months (11.7–19.3) with standard radiation plus TMZ, 14.5 months (12.9–15.9) with TMZ chemoradiation plus TMZ, and 2.7 months (1.2–5.4) with unspecified radiation. Across all groups, survival curves for methylated tumors cluster at higher survival times, indicating that MGMT promoter methylation confers both a prognostic advantage and increased benefit from TMZ-containing therapy. Haiku 5.5 · ✓ Pass 41s · 318,733 tok · $0.009 · 7 LLM / 11 tool calls · transcript

In the TCGA PanCancer Atlas breast cancer study, the correlation between EGFR mRNA expression and PIK3CA mutation status among the 55 samples with a PTEN homozygous deletion is **r = −0.085**. That is a very weak negative correlation.

| Measure | Value |
|---|---|
| Samples with PTEN homozygous deletion and EGFR expression | 55 |
| PIK3CA mutated | 6 |
| PIK3CA wild-type | 49 |
| Mean EGFR expression, PIK3CA mutated | 356.6 |
| Mean EGFR expression, PIK3CA wild-type | 515.9 |

- **Method:** I used the raw RSEM mRNA profile (`rna_seq_v2_mrna`), not the z-scored one. PIK3CA mutation status was coded 1 for mutated and 0 for not mutated, and I computed a Pearson correlation with that binary variable (point-biserial). Any PIK3CA mutation call other than "UNCALLED" counted as mutated.
- **Caveats:** Only 6 samples carry the PIK3CA mutation, so the estimate is unstable. I did not compute a p-value. For this sample size, a correlation of this magnitude would not be expected to be significant, but you should confirm that with a proper test in R or Python. The Group Comparison tab in cBioPortal can also run a two-group expression comparison.

I also prepared a StudyView link filtered to PTEN deep-deletion samples: [View PTEN homozygous deletion cohort](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018#filterJson=%7B%22geneFilters%22%3A%5B%7B%22geneQueries%22%3A%5B%5B%7B%22alterations%22%3A%5B%22HOMDEL%22%5D%2C%22hugoGeneSymbol%22%3A%22PTEN%22%7D%5D%5D%2C%22molecularProfileIds%22%3A%5B%22brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018_gistic%22%5D%7D%5D%7D)

Haiku 5.5 · ✓ Pass 56s · 358,156 tok · $0.010 · 6 LLM / 10 tool calls · transcript

In the TCGA PanCancer Atlas breast cancer study (`brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018`), patients with at least two of the five most frequently mutated genes have a mean TMB of **5.53 mutations/Mb**, about twice the cohort mean of **2.73 mutations/Mb**.

**The five most frequently mutated genes** (patients with a mutation, out of 1,066 profiled):
- PIK3CA: 347 (32.6%)
- TP53: 347 (32.6%)
- TTN: 187 (17.5%)
- CDH1: 130 (12.2%)
- GATA3: 127 (11.9%)

**TMB comparison** (`TMB_NONSYNONYMOUS`, patient level; 1,066 of 1,084 patients have a TMB value):

| Group | Patients | Mean TMB | Median TMB |
|---|---|---|---|
| ≥2 of the top 5 genes | 296 (27.8%) | 5.53 | 2.03 |
| 0–1 of the top 5 genes | 770 | 1.66 | 1.17 |
| All patients with TMB | 1,066 | 2.73 | 1.30 |

The mean is pulled up by a subset of hypermutated tumors, so the median is the more typical value. Even so, the median is about 1.7 times higher in the ≥2 group (2.03 vs 1.17).

Two caveats:
- **Circularity:** TTN, PIK3CA, TP53, CDH1 and GATA3 are themselves counted in TMB, so part of the difference comes from these genes. Excluding them would give a cleaner comparison.
- **No significance testing:** I can't run a statistical test here, so the difference is descriptive only. A Wilcoxon test on the two groups would be the appropriate check, and cBioPortal's Group Comparison page can run one.

[View OncoPrint for these genes with TMB track](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/oncoprint?cancer_study_list=brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018&gene_list=TP53%0ATTN%0ACDH1&case_set_id=brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018_all&clinicallist=TMB_NONSYNONYMOUS)

The Pan-Lung Cancer TCGA study (`nsclc_tcga_broad_2016`, 1,144 samples) splits cleanly by histology into 660 lung adenocarcinomas (LUAD) and 484 squamous cell carcinomas (LUSC). The strongest differences are in the oncogenic drivers: LUAD is dominated by KRAS, EGFR and STK11 alterations, while LUSC carries far more TP53 and squamous-typical alterations (NFE2L2, PTEN, PIK3CA, KMT2D, CDKN2A).

**Mutation frequency by histology** (samples with a mutation in the gene / samples profiled, counts are samples, non-silent mutations only)

| Gene | LUAD (n=660) | LUSC (n=484) | Pattern |
|---|---|---|---|
| TP53 | 357 (54.1%) | 418 (86.4%) | Higher in LUSC |
| KRAS | 214 (32.4%) | 8 (1.7%) | LUAD-enriched |
| EGFR | 104 (15.8%) | 13 (2.7%) | LUAD-enriched |
| STK11 | 104 (15.8%) | 7 (1.4%) | LUAD-enriched |
| KEAP1 | 110 (16.7%) | 52 (10.7%) | LUAD-enriched |
| RBM10 | 41 (6.2%) | 6 (1.2%) | LUAD-enriched |
| SMARCA4 | 61 (9.2%) | 18 (3.7%) | LUAD-enriched |
| BRAF | 54 (8.2%) | 16 (3.3%) | LUAD-enriched |
| MET | 28 (4.2%) | 6 (1.2%) | LUAD-enriched |
| KMT2D | 49 (7.4%) | 114 (23.6%) | Higher in LUSC |
| NFE2L2 | 14 (2.1%) | 70 (14.5%) | Higher in LUSC |
| PTEN | 11 (1.7%) | 56 (11.6%) | Higher in LUSC |
| PIK3CA | 39 (5.9%) | 55 (11.4%) | Higher in LUSC |
| NOTCH1 | 31 (4.7%) | 40 (8.3%) | Higher in LUSC |
| CDKN2A | 27 (4.1%) | 75 (15.5%) | Higher in LUSC (mutations only) |
| RB1 | 38 (5.8%) | 35 (7.2%) | Similar |
| NF1 | 76 (11.5%) | 51 (10.5%) | Similar |
| FAT1 | 73 (11.1%) | 69 (14.3%) | Similar |
| CSMD3 | 262 (39.7%) | 215 (44.4%) | Similar |

**Key differences**
- **LUAD:** Oncogenic drivers are largely mutually distinct from one another. KRAS, EGFR, BRAF, MET and ERBB2 mutations, together with STK11, KEAP1 and SMARCA4 alterations, are concentrated in LUAD.
- **LUSC:** TP53 is mutated in over 85% of tumours. Squamous-associated genes are enriched: NFE2L2 (KEAP1 pathway), PTEN, PIK3CA, KMT2D, NOTCH1 and CDKN2A.
- **Shared:** NF1, FAT1 and CSMD3 occur at similar frequencies in both histologies.

**Caveats**
- These are mutation-only results. I did not analyse copy-number changes or fusions (e.g., ALK, RET, ROS1 rearrangements), which are also known to differ by histology. CDKN2A in particular is likely underestimated.
- The TCGA data here has no driver annotation, so these are all non-silent mutations, not confirmed drivers.
- I have not run significance tests. The differences above are descriptive; for p-values, use the Mutations comparison tab linked below.

[Compare LUAD vs LUSC mutation frequencies in cBioPortal](https://www.cbioportal.org/comparison/mutations?comparisonId=6ab4329ee497aad53593be0b)

Source
https://github.com/cBioPortal/cbioportal-mcp/issues/20

Haiku 5.5 · ✗ Fail 16s · 113,291 tok · $0.004 · 3 LLM / 3 tool calls · transcript

**Short answer:** cBioPortal already stores and visualizes germline variants alongside somatic ones, but germline support is per-study and has real gaps for classification and somatic-focused analyses. I can't confirm any future roadmap from the database or its guides, and I haven't verified how other tools compare.

**Current support**

- **Storage:** Germline calls are stored in the same mutation data as somatic calls and distinguished by a free-text `mutation_status` field. Studies spell it differently (`Germline`, `GERMLINE`, `germline`), so queries need case-insensitive matching.
- **Coverage is study-specific.** Many studies drop germline calls during processing. In this database, 16 studies currently have germline mutation records. The largest by record count are:
  - [coadread_tcga_pub](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=coadread_tcga_pub) (101)
  - [lung_nci_2022](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=lung_nci_2022) (100)
  - [pog570_bcgsc_2020](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=pog570_bcgsc_2020) (74)
  - [coadread_tcga](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=coadread_tcga) (70)
  - [hnsc_a5consortium_2025](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=hnsc_a5consortium_2025) (67)

  These are mutation-record counts, not patient counts.
- **Features that work for germline:** study view, clinical correlation, OncoPrint, mutation diagrams, group comparison, and frequency queries when filtered by mutation status.
- **Gaps:**
  - Driver, OncoKB, and TMB features are designed for somatic variants. Mutual exclusivity analysis is also typically applied to somatic alterations.
  - Pathogenic, likely pathogenic, or VUS classifications are not stored in standard fields.
  - Some public instances exclude germline data for privacy reasons.
  - Many studies leave somatic calls unannotated (`NA`, `UNKNOWN`), so germline status isn't always clear.

**Future support**

I can't find planned germline features in the database or the guides, so I can't say what is coming. The cBioPortal release notes and documentation are the best places to check.

**Alternatives (general knowledge, not from cBioPortal data)**

- Dedicated variant-interpretation resources such as ClinVar, which holds clinical significance assertions, and gnomAD, which provides population allele frequencies, are the usual places to check classification and population frequency. cBioPortal doesn't do either.
- Repositories such as the GDC provide raw sequencing files, which cBioPortal does not store.
- Clinical genetics reporting systems handle germline interpretation and return of results. cBioPortal is an exploratory visualization tool for processed data, not a substitute for those.
The answer correctly notes that cBioPortal supports germline variants via mutation_status and that features like study view, clinical correlation, and OncoPrint work for germline data, avoiding the "somatic-only" error. However, the notes require the answer to point to documentation on building a germline study, which the answer does not do — it only mentions release notes generally. This missing required element means the answer fails the criteria.

**Short answer:** cBioPortal can't tell you whether TP53 mutations are clinically actionable. The database has no actionability tiers, and its only driver annotations don't cover TP53 in a clinically useful way.

**What the database holds for TP53:**
- About 149,000 TP53 mutation records have no driver annotation at all.
- Only about 1,300 are labelled `Putative_Driver`, and those labels come from clonal hematopoiesis studies (`msk_ch_*`) using their own criteria ("CH-PD", "Pathogenic or Likely-Pathogenic", and similar).
- The `alteration_driver_annotation` table has OncoKB-style tier fields, but every row is empty.
- OncoKB oncogenicity and therapeutic levels are computed by the cBioPortal web app at view time and aren't stored here.

**What you can check in cBioPortal:** the OncoKB-annotated view is available in the portal's results pages with the OQL `TP53: MUT_DRIVER` (or `TP53: DRIVER`). Once you pick a study, I can generate that link. The OncoKB site itself lists the therapeutic evidence for specific alterations.

**General background (not from cBioPortal data, so verify against OncoKB or the primary literature):** TP53 is a tumor suppressor. To my knowledge, no targeted therapy is approved specifically for TP53 mutations. Some clinical trials target the TP53 pathway, for example MDM2 inhibitors in TP53 wild-type tumors. Actionability depends on the specific variant, the cancer type, and the therapy.

This is research information, not clinical advice. For any patient-level decision, please work with a clinical molecular tumor board.

#78 Out of scopeOut of scope · All Studieswhat's the best way to put clinical data into cbioportal? ✗12s
Notes
A correct answer must: describe the options: submitting to the public portal (curation team, cBioPortal/datahub, file formats), running a private instance, and visualizing own data without submission (OncoPrinter, MutationMapper); ask which use case applies. Must not: say it cannot help.
Source
https://github.com/cBioPortal/cbioportal-mcp/issues/26

Haiku 5.5 · ✗ Fail 12s · 33,726 tok · $0.002 · 1 LLM / 0 tool calls · transcript

The best route depends on whether you're loading into a local cBioPortal instance or submitting a study for the public portal. In both cases the work is in the file format, and the import tooling validates it.

**1. Prepare the clinical files**

A study needs two clinical files, one per level:
- `data_clinical_patient.txt`: one row per patient (e.g., `SEX`, `AGE`, `OS_MONTHS`, `OS_STATUS`).
- `data_clinical_sample.txt`: one row per sample (e.g., `ONCOTREE_CODE`, `SAMPLE_TYPE`), with `PATIENT_ID` linking back to the patient file.

Each file starts with four header lines, followed by a column-name line and then the data:

```
#Patient Identifier	Sex	Overall Survival (Months)	Overall Survival Status
#Patient identifier	Sex of the patient	Overall survival in months	Overall survival status
#STRING	STRING	NUMBER	STRING
#1	1	1	1
PATIENT_ID	SEX	OS_MONTHS	OS_STATUS
P001	Female	34.2	0:LIVING
```

The `#datatype` line must be `STRING`, `NUMBER`, or `BOOLEAN`. Use tab-separated values and no spaces in the column names.

**2. Add a meta file for each data file**

`meta_clinical_patient.txt` and `meta_clinical_sample.txt` tell the importer what each file is:

```
cancer_study_identifier: my_study_2026
genetic_alteration_type: CLINICAL
datatype: PATIENT_ATTRIBUTES
data_filename: data_clinical_patient.txt
```

Use `SAMPLE_ATTRIBUTES` for the sample file.

**3. Follow the conventions that matter most**
- Use the standard attribute names where they exist (e.g., `OS_MONTHS`/`OS_STATUS`, `DFS_MONTHS`/`DFS_STATUS`, `AGE`, `SEX`). Standard names let the portal's survival and clinical views work automatically.
- Survival status uses the `0:LIVING` / `1:DECEASED` style, not free text.
- Cancer type should be an OncoTree code in `ONCOTREE_CODE`. Look codes up with the OncoTree site or the search tool here.
- Sample and patient IDs must match exactly across the clinical files and any mutation or CNA files.
- De-identify ages and dates before loading, and check the portal's own guidance on what is allowed.

**4. Validate and import**

- **Local instance:** Run the cBioPortal importer on the study folder. Its validator reports format and ID-matching errors before anything loads. The `cbioportal` GitHub documentation covers the exact command for your version (Docker or the Java/Perl importer).
- **Public portal:** Studies are submitted through the cBioPortal data hub on GitHub (a pull request with the files and meta files) and go through the same validation.

**For MSK:** If the data is internal, check with the MSK cBioPortal team about the institutional ingestion path before loading anything, because data-sharing and de-identification rules may apply.

If you tell me what the clinical data looks like (columns, whether it's patient- or sample-level, and whether it's for a local instance or the public portal), I can help draft the two data files and meta files.
The answer provides detailed guidance on submitting clinical data to cBioPortal, covering both local instances and the public portal (via GitHub datahub), and asks which use case applies at the end. However, it omits mentioning OncoPrinter and MutationMapper as options for visualizing one's own data without full submission, which is a required element per the notes. The answer does not incorrectly decline or say it cannot help.

| Study | Samples with MYC expression | KRAS-mutant | KRAS-mutant, TP53 wild-type | …and MYC z > 1 | …and MYC z > 2 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| [Lung Adenocarcinoma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=luad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018) | 510 | 154 | 100 | 13 (13%) | 2 (2%) |

| **Combined** | 994 | 161 | 104 | 13 (12.5%) | 2 (1.9%) |

- **Counting unit:** samples. These studies have one sample per patient, so the numbers should match patients.
- **Definitions:** KRAS-mutant means at least one KRAS mutation call. TP53 wild-type means no TP53 mutation call; copy-number losses of TP53 are not excluded. High MYC means the within-study z-score of MYC mRNA (RNA-seq, all-sample normalization) is above 1 or above 2. I chose these cutoffs; the portal's default for expression thresholds is not something I've confirmed.
- **Study choice:** the 2016 pan-lung TCGA study has no MYC expression data, so I excluded it. The LUAD study accounts for almost all of the matching samples.
- **Caveat:** these are descriptive counts only. I did not run a statistical test, and the z-scores are relative to each study, not absolute expression levels.

## IDH1 mutations in glioma

IDH1 mutations are common in diffuse glioma, but the frequency depends heavily on which cohort you look at. Each study below is a separate cohort, so the figures are not pooled.

| Study | Altered / profiled | Frequency |
|---|---|---|

- **R132H:** 358 samples (69.6% of 514 profiled), by far the dominant variant

- **R132S:** 9 (1.8%)

All IDH1 mutations in this cohort fall at codon 132. R132H is the classic IDH1 hotspot; that point is general background knowledge, not something this database annotates.

**Caveats**
- Several of these studies probably share patients. The two TCGA glioma cohorts, for example, likely overlap, so do not add their counts together.

#88 Out of scopeOut of scope · All Studieswhat do idh1 mutations do? ✓9s
Notes
A correct answer must: clearly separate what comes from cBioPortal data from general background knowledge, and offer cBioPortal-grounded lookups (frequencies, co-mutations, outcomes). Must not: present general biology as if it came from cBioPortal, without a disclaimer.
Source
https://github.com/cBioPortal/cbioportal-mcp/issues/57

Haiku 5.5 · ✓ Pass 9s · 33,351 tok · $0.001 · 1 LLM / 0 tool calls · transcript

**IDH1** (isocitrate dehydrogenase 1) is a cytoplasmic enzyme that normally converts isocitrate to α-ketoglutarate (α-KG) while producing NADPH. Cancer-associated IDH1 mutations are typically heterozygous, change a single arginine in the active site (most often R132, with R132H the most common), and give the enzyme a new activity.

**What the mutations do:**

1. **Neomorphic enzyme activity:** The mutant enzyme reduces α-KG to **D-2-hydroxyglutarate (D-2HG)**, which accumulates to very high levels in tumor cells.
2. **Oncometabolite effect:** D-2HG structurally resembles α-KG and inhibits α-KG-dependent dioxygenases, including TET DNA demethylases and Jumonji-family histone demethylases.
3. **Epigenetic reprogramming:** Inhibiting these enzymes produces widespread DNA and histone hypermethylation (a CpG island methylator phenotype). This is thought to block cell differentiation, which contributes to tumorigenesis.

**Where they are common:** IDH1 mutations are recurrent in diffuse gliomas, acute myeloid leukemia, and cholangiocarcinoma. In gliomas, IDH-mutant tumors form a distinct molecular class and are generally associated with better prognosis than IDH-wildtype tumors.

This summary is general biology knowledge, not data from cBioPortal, and I haven't checked it against the database. I can query cBioPortal to show how often IDH1 is mutated across cancer types, which variants occur (e.g., R132H vs. other residues), and which studies have the data. Would you like that?

Before I query, I need to confirm what "not a point mutation" should include. The term is ambiguous, and it can be read several ways that give different answers:

1. **Indels only:** in-frame and frameshift insertions/deletions (`In_Frame_Ins`, `In_Frame_Del`, `Frame_Shift_Ins`, `Frame_Shift_Del`).

Haiku 5.5 · ✗ Fail 18s · 71,017 tok · $0.003 · 2 LLM / 1 tool calls · transcript

I can't check the paper's text from here, so this is from my general knowledge of the study and from the database's study guide. Verify specifics against the Methods and Extended Data before relying on them.

**Potential methodological concerns**

1. **NLP-derived variables.** Several clinical fields are extracted from free text, including treatment, radiology progression, and some biomarker status. The database guide flags these as NLP-derived with possible extraction errors. Accuracy may differ by cancer type, institution period, or note style, so any association involving these variables could carry differential misclassification.

2. **Missing treatment equals no treatment.** The guide notes that the data cannot distinguish "not collected" from "not received." Treatment-based analyses, such as immunotherapy versus none, are therefore vulnerable to misclassifying untreated or unrecorded patients.

3. **Immunotherapy response is proxied.** The database has no RECIST or best-response field. Outcomes rely on overall survival or NLP-derived progression events. Those proxies are coarser than trial-grade response data and are sensitive to how "line of therapy" and treatment start are defined.

4. **Confounding in observational survival and treatment comparisons.** This is a retrospective, single-institution cohort. Treatment choice tracks performance status, stage, and molecular features. Multivariable adjustment can only address measured confounders, and the paper's conclusions about biomarkers and outcomes should be read with that limit in mind.

5. **Targeted-panel heterogeneity.** The cohort spans four MSK-IMPACT versions (341, 410, 468, and 505 genes). Gene coverage differs by sample. Genes added in later panels have shorter observation windows, so frequency comparisons across time need gene-specific denominators. TMB calculated on different panel sizes is noisier on smaller panels and needs normalization.

6. **Unit of analysis.** The guide reports 24,950 patients and 25,040 samples, with 90 multi-sample patients, all from different cancer types. Pooling samples as independent observations can inflate effective sample size. Any analysis that counts samples rather than patients should say which unit it uses.

7. **Pooled co-occurrence and mutual exclusivity.** Pan-cancer co-mutation tests can show Simpson's-paradox effects, because some gene pairs are enriched in particular cancer types. Results should be stratified by cancer type, with multiple-testing correction across many gene pairs.

8. **Selection bias.** Patients were sequenced at a referral center, often at later stages or after clinical need. The cohort is not a population sample, and its ancestry and demographic composition may limit generalizability.

9. **Time origin and guarantee-time bias.** Survival time may start at diagnosis, at sequencing, or at treatment start. Those choices change the comparison, and patients must survive long enough to receive a treatment, which can bias treatment-versus-outcome comparisons.

10. **Germline and somatic classification.** Germline filtering depends on matched normals and on pipeline versions that changed over the study period. Residual germline variants can distort variant allele fraction and driver-gene analyses.

11. **Reproducibility.** The data are licensed CC BY-NC-ND and require a request for commercial use. Independent reanalysis is therefore constrained, which matters given the NLP-derived variables.

**What would strengthen the study:** a manual-review validation subset with reported NLP accuracy per variable, sensitivity analyses that treat unknown treatment as a separate category, and per-cancer-type reporting of co-occurrence and survival results.

If you want, I can pull the cohort composition or treatment-coverage numbers directly from the database to check any of these points.
The answer directly critiques the MSK-CHORD paper's methodology in extensive detail, which is exactly what the notes say it must not do. The question is out of scope for cBioPortal, and the correct response should have declined to critique the paper's methodology and offered to explore the cBioPortal data instead. Instead, the answer produces a lengthy list of methodological concerns, violating the "must not: critique the paper's methodology" requirement.

## Expected drivers in adenoid cystic carcinoma (ACYC)

I used the largest cohort, **Adenoid Cystic Carcinoma Project (`acc_2019`)**, with 1,049 samples from 1,045 patients. Other ACYC studies overlap with it and could double-count patients, so I did not pool them. Mutation frequencies below are patient-level (altered patients / 1,045). The database has no driver or OncoKB tier annotations for these studies, so the actionability tiers are my general knowledge, not database output.

**Fusions (structural variants, sample-level, 1,049 samples)**
- **MYB** fusions: 17.8%
- **NFIB** fusions: 16.5%
- **MYBL1** fusions: 0.7%

MYB and NFIB fusions are the hallmark ACYC events. They are not standard mutations, so they do not appear in the mutation counts.

**Somatic mutations (patients)**

**Summary by actionability (general knowledge)**

- **Approved in other cancers for the same alteration (weakest evidence for ACYC):** PIK3CA, KIT, ERBB2, and NTRK-fusion-directed therapy if NTRK fusions were present.
- **Investigational, pathway-level:** NOTCH1 and SPEN (NOTCH pathway), TSC2 and PIK3CA (mTOR/PI3K), and MYB/NFIB fusions (no direct inhibitor).

## BCOR in ACYC

**Somatic:** BCOR is the fifth most frequently mutated gene in `acc_2019`, with 109 patients (10.4%) and 119 mutation events. Most events are truncating (frameshift, nonsense, or splice), and a smaller number are missense. The other ACYC studies also have BCOR mutations, but only a handful each (for example, one event in `acyc_mda_2015` and one in `acyc_mskcc_2013`).

**Germline:** I found no germline BCOR or BCORL1 calls in any study in the database. Every BCOR call in the ACYC studies is labelled somatic (or unannotated). Many studies do not load germline calls, so this does not prove that no germline BCOR variants exist in ACYC patients.

Germline BCOR variants are linked to oculofaciocardiodental (OFCD) syndrome. That is general knowledge, not drawn from these data.
The reference requires restricting data to salivary adenoid cystic carcinoma (OncoTree ACYC, 935 of 1,049 samples), but the answer uses the full acc_2019 cohort of 1,049 samples without filtering to ONCOTREE_CODE=ACYC. Haiku 5.5 · ✗ Fail 31s · 116,894 tok · $0.006 · 3 LLM / 4 tool calls · transcript

Here's a standard-library script (no pandas needed) that reads the MSK-CHORD timeline files and supports summaries, per-patient timelines, and filtered queries.

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Query MSK-CHORD (msk_chord_2024) timeline files.

Timeline files (data_timeline*.txt) are tab-separated, long-format event tables:
    PATIENT_ID  START_DATE  STOP_DATE  EVENT_TYPE  
START_DATE / STOP_DATE are days relative to diagnosis. Lines beginning with '#'
are metadata and are skipped. Event types in this study include Diagnosis,
Lab_Test, Pathology, Sample acquisition, Sequencing, Surgery, and Treatment.

Examples:
    python query_chord_timeline.py /path/to/msk_chord_2024 --summary
    python query_chord_timeline.py /path/to/msk_chord_2024 --patient P-0000001
    python query_chord_timeline.py /path/to/msk_chord_2024 --event-type Treatment \
        --where SUBTYPE=Immuno --count-patients
    python query_chord_timeline.py /path/to/msk_chord_2024 --event-type Treatment \
        --where SUBTYPE=Chemo --where AGENT=FLUOROURACIL --limit 20
"""
import argparse
import csv
import glob
import os
import sys
from collections import Counter, defaultdict


def find_timeline_files(study_dir):
    """Return all data_timeline*.txt files in the study directory."""
    files = sorted(glob.glob(os.path.join(study_dir, "data_timeline*.txt")))
    if not files:
        sys.exit(f"No data_timeline*.txt files found in {study_dir}")
    return files


def load_timeline_files(paths):
    """Read timeline files into a list of dicts, one dict per event row."""
    rows = []
    for path in paths:
        with open(path, newline="", encoding="utf-8") as fh:
            data_lines = (line for line in fh if not line.startswith("#"))
            for rec in csv.DictReader(data_lines, delimiter="\t"):
                clean = {k.strip(): (v or "").strip() for k, v in rec.items() if k}
                clean["_source_file"] = os.path.basename(path)
                rows.append(clean)
    return rows


def _to_float(value):
    try:
        return float(value)
    except (TypeError, ValueError):
        return None


def _start_key(row):
    """Sort key: events by START_DATE, undated events last."""
    start = _to_float(row.get("START_DATE"))
    return (start is None, start if start is not None else 0.0)


def filter_rows(rows, patient=None, event_type=None, where=None):
    """Filter events. `where` is a dict of {COLUMN: value}; comparison is case-insensitive."""
    out = []
    for r in rows:
        if patient and r.get("PATIENT_ID") != patient:
            continue
        if event_type and r.get("EVENT_TYPE", "").casefold() != event_type.casefold():
            continue
        if where and any(r.get(col, "").casefold() != val.casefold()
                         for col, val in where.items()):
            continue
        out.append(r)
    return out


def summarize(rows):
    """Print event and patient counts per EVENT_TYPE, plus SUBTYPE counts where present."""
    n_events = Counter(r.get("EVENT_TYPE", "") for r in rows)
    patients = defaultdict(set)
    subtypes = defaultdict(Counter)
    for r in rows:
        et = r.get("EVENT_TYPE", "")
        patients[et].add(r.get("PATIENT_ID", ""))
        if r.get("SUBTYPE"):
            subtypes[et][r["SUBTYPE"]] += 1

    print(f"{'EVENT_TYPE':<22}{'events':>10}{'patients':>10}")
    for et, n in n_events.most_common():
        print(f"{et:<22}{n:>10}{len(patients[et]):>10}")
        for st, sn in subtypes[et].most_common():
            print(f"    {st:<18}{sn:>10}")
    total_patients = len({r.get('PATIENT_ID', '') for r in rows})
    print(f"\nTotal events: {len(rows)}   Distinct patients: {total_patients}")


def print_rows(rows, limit):
    """Print events in chronological order per patient, tab-separated."""
    if not rows:
        print("No matching events.")
        return
    rows = sorted(rows, key=lambda r: (r.get("PATIENT_ID", ""), _start_key(r)))
    cols = ["PATIENT_ID", "START_DATE", "STOP_DATE", "EVENT_TYPE"]
    extra = sorted({k for r in rows for k in r} - set(cols) - {"_source_file"})
    cols += extra
    print("\t".join(cols))
    for r in rows[:limit]:
        print("\t".join(r.get(c, "") for c in cols))
    if len(rows) > limit:
        print(f"... {len(rows) - limit} more rows (raise --limit to see them)")


def parse_where(items):
    where = {}
    for item in items:
        col, sep, val = item.partition("=")
        if not sep or not col or not val:
            sys.exit(f"--where must be COLUMN=VALUE, got: {item!r}")
        where[col.strip()] = val.strip()
    return where


def main():
    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="Query MSK-CHORD timeline files.")
    ap.add_argument("study_dir", help="Directory containing the msk_chord_2024 data files")
    ap.add_argument("--files", nargs="+", help="Specific timeline files (default: all data_timeline*.txt)")
    ap.add_argument("--summary", action="store_true", help="Counts by event type and subtype")
    ap.add_argument("--patient", help="Show the timeline for one PATIENT_ID")
    ap.add_argument("--event-type", help="Restrict to an EVENT_TYPE (e.g. Treatment, Surgery)")
    ap.add_argument("--where", action="append", default=[], metavar="COL=VALUE",
                    help="Exact-match column filter (repeatable), e.g. --where SUBTYPE=Immuno")
    ap.add_argument("--count-patients", action="store_true", help="Print distinct patient count only")
    ap.add_argument("--limit", type=int, default=50, help="Max rows to print (default 50)")
    args = ap.parse_args()

    paths = args.files or find_timeline_files(args.study_dir)
    rows = load_timeline_files(paths)
    print(f"Loaded {len(rows)} events from {len(paths)} file(s): "
          f"{', '.join(os.path.basename(p) for p in paths)}\n", file=sys.stderr)

    selected = filter_rows(rows, patient=args.patient, event_type=args.event_type,
                           where=parse_where(args.where))

    if args.summary:
        summarize(selected)
    elif args.count_patients:
        print(len({r.get("PATIENT_ID", "") for r in selected}))
    else:
        print_rows(selected, args.limit)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

**Usage notes**

- Save the script outside the data directory and pass the data path as an argument.
- Column names beyond `PATIENT_ID`, `START_DATE`, `STOP_DATE`, and `EVENT_TYPE` vary by event type, so use `--summary` first to see what is present. Treatment events, for example, carry `SUBTYPE`, `AGENT`, and `RX_INVESTIGATIVE`.
- `--count-patients` counts distinct patients, which is usually what you want for cohort numbers. Event counts can be much larger than patient counts.
- The file name pattern `data_timeline*.txt` is the usual cBioPortal convention. If your download uses different names, pass them with `--files`.
- For survival or time-to-event analysis, the script doesn't compute Kaplan-Meier estimates. Export the filtered events and use R or Python `lifelines`, or the cBioPortal Survival tab.
- MSK-CHORD is licensed CC BY-NC-ND 4.0. Commercial use requires permission from datarequests@mskcc.org.
The notes require the code to query the public cBioPortal REST API (e.g., /api/studies/{studyId}/clinical-events). The answer instead writes code that reads local flat files (data_timeline*.txt) from a downloaded study directory, bypassing the API entirely. This fails the "must" requirement even though it avoids ClickHouse credentials.

**PIK3CA p.\*1069Wext\*3 is not in the database as written.** The closest match is **p.\*1069Wext\*4**, which is a stop-loss (extension) variant. Your report may use a different extension-length convention, or it may be a typo, so please check the source.

**What the database holds for this variant (`*1069Wext*4`)**
- **Mutation type:** Nonstop_Mutation (a stop-loss variant, where the normal stop codon is replaced by an amino acid and translation continues)
- **Total:** 27 samples across 17 studies (counted as samples, not patients; each sample had one event)
- **Tumour samples:** about 18 across 12 studies. Several are glioblastoma entries from overlapping TCGA releases (e.g. `gbm_tcga`, `gbm_tcga_gdc`, `gbm_tcga_pub2013`, `gbm_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018`), which may be the same patients.
- **Cell lines:** 9 samples (CCLE and NCI-60 studies)
- **Largest tumour contributors:** `msk_impact_50k_2026` (3), and 2 each in `msk_met_2021`, `msk_chord_2024`, `crc_msk_2026`, and `pancan_mimsi_msk_2024`

**Related PIK3CA variants at the same codon**
The same stop codon is also recorded with frameshift and other notations, for example `*1069Ffs*5` (Frame_Shift_Ins, roughly 30 samples), `*1069*` (Nonstop_Mutation, about 6 samples), and `*1069fs*` (Frame_Shift_Del, 2 samples). These are different events at the same position.

**Limitations**
- The database does not store OncoKB or other clinical oncogenicity annotations for this variant, and I have not checked its driver status, so I can't say whether it is oncogenic.
- The protein-level interpretation is general knowledge, not from cBioPortal data. PIK3CA's native protein is about 1068 residues, so the stop codon at 1069 sits at the C-terminus. Losing it extends the protein by a short tail.

If you can share the report's source or sample IDs, I can check which samples carry this variant and in which study.

Haiku 5.5 · ✓ Pass 61s · 627,850 tok · $0.021 · 8 LLM / 15 tool calls · transcript

## Mutation frequency by tumor sidedness in colorectal cancer

Across four colorectal cohorts that record sidedness, several genes show a consistent pattern. Right-sided tumors have a higher observed frequency of BRAF, PIK3CA, KRAS, SMAD4, FBXW7, PTEN, and ATM mutations, while left-sided tumors have a higher frequency of TP53 and APC mutations. These are descriptive counts. I did not run significance tests, so treat the differences as hypotheses to test.

**Method:** Each study is reported separately because the sidedness fields and cohorts differ. Frequencies are sample-level: altered samples ÷ samples profiled for that gene, excluding UNCALLED and off-panel mutations. Tumors coded as neither left nor right (e.g., transverse) were excluded.

| Gene | TCGA Nature 2012 (`coadread_tcga_pub`) L / R | MSK 2017 (`crc_msk_2017`) L / R | MSK APC-IMPACT 2020 (`crc_apc_impact_2020`) L / R | SYSUCC 2022 (`crc_sysucc_2022`) L / R |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| TP53 | **63.5%** (47/74) / 34.8% (23/66) | **79.2%** (616/778) / 58.8% (201/342) | **75.6%** (146/193) / 65.1% (99/152) | **48.2%** (335/695) / 38.4% (123/320) |
| APC | **79.7%** / 63.6% | **81.6%** / 65.2% | **71.5%** / 63.8% | **46.8%** / 39.4% |
| BRAF | 1.4% (1/74) / **24.2%** (16/66) | 6.9% (54/778) / **22.8%** (78/342) | 10.9% (21/193) / **20.4%** (31/152) | 2.4% (17/695) / **8.1%** (26/320) |
| PIK3CA | 10.8% / **27.3%** | 15.9% / **29.5%** | 18.1% / **23.7%** | 7.9% / **23.4%** |
| KRAS | 28.4% / **45.5%** | 37.7% / **56.7%** | 40.9% / **60.5%** | 21.6% / **27.8%** |
| SMAD4 | 8.1% / **15.2%** | 12.9% / **20.5%** | 15.0% / **17.1%** | 6.3% / **10.9%** |
| FBXW7 | 12.2% / **22.7%** | 11.7% / **15.5%** | 7.3% / **13.2%** | 11.4% / **13.1%** |
| PTEN | 2.7% / 3.0% | 3.6% / **11.4%** | 4.1% / **8.6%** | 2.3% / **9.7%** |
| ATM | 8.1% / **21.2%** | 5.3% / **12.9%** | 5.7% / **7.9%** | 4.3% / **10.3%** |
| NRAS | 10.8% / 7.6% | 4.0% / 4.4% | 3.1% / 2.6% | 2.6% / 1.9% |

Bold marks the higher value in each row. Counts for the other studies are in parentheses where shown. NRAS shows no consistent difference.

**Patterns:**
- **BRAF** is the clearest signal. Right-sided tumors have roughly 3–10 times the frequency of left-sided tumors in every cohort.
- **TP53 and APC** are higher on the left in all four cohorts.
- **KRAS, PIK3CA, and SMAD4** are higher on the right in all four cohorts.

**Caveats:**
- The sidedness definition varies by study, and the cohorts are heterogeneous. The MSK cohorts are metastatic or mixed-stage, and TCGA is primary tumors. Pooling across studies would risk double-counting overlapping MSK patients, so I did not combine them.
- Sample-level counts were used. These cohorts are mostly one sample per patient, but I did not check this explicitly.
- `crc_msk_2026` (the largest colorectal study) and the TCGA PanCancer Atlas colorectal study do not have a sidedness field in the database, so they are not included.

To test these differences formally, the Group Comparison page for the MSK 2017 cohort compares left- and right-sided tumors using the mutations view:

- CHST9: +2.2
- IRX1: +2.2

- IRX2: +2.0
- MSTN: +2.0
- Fewer genes pass the same threshold here than in the other two subtypes.

**Caveats**

**Observations**

- EGFR expression is highest in glioblastoma, head and neck, clear cell renal, glioma, and lung adenocarcinoma/squamous cell carcinoma cohorts. Glioblastoma has the highest 90th percentile (15.00), which suggests a tail of very high-expressing tumors.
- Breast cancer has a large sample size (1,082) but a below-median expression level.
- Leukemia (AML) has the lowest expression, with a median near zero.
- Some cancer types, such as bladder, have a much higher 90th percentile than median. That pattern points to a subset of high-expressing tumors rather than uniformly high expression.

These are descriptive summaries. The database doesn't run statistical tests, so I haven't reported any significance values.

**EGFR point mutations in lung cancer: TCGA PanCancer Atlas cohorts**

I've taken "point mutations" to mean single-residue protein changes: missense, nonsense, and splice-site/splice-region calls. In-frame insertions and deletions are excluded. Tell me if you want a different definition, such as including the in-frame indels (which are common in EGFR) or a different set of studies.

**Lung adenocarcinoma (LUAD), TCGA PanCancer Atlas: 566 profiled samples**
- Missense: 41 samples (53 mutation events)
- Nonsense: 2 samples
- Splice region: 1 sample
- Total point-type: about 44 samples, or 7.8%
- Most frequent protein changes: L858R (23 samples), L861Q (3), G719A, L62R, S768I, T790M (2 each). The rest are singletons.

**Lung squamous cell carcinoma (LUSC), TCGA PanCancer Atlas: 484 profiled samples**
- Missense: 9 samples
- Splice region: 2 samples
- Splice site: 1 sample
- Nonsense: 1 sample
- Total point-type: about 13 samples, or 2.7%
- Most frequent: L861Q (2 samples). The rest are singletons.

**Other lung cohorts (counts only, no frequency calculated)**
- Lung Adenocarcinoma (MSK, 2017; `lung_msk_2017`): missense in 140 patients. The most common changes were L858R (70 patients) and T790M (47). Pooling counts across MSK lung studies risks double-counting shared patients, so I've kept them separate.
- Lung Adenocarcinoma (MSK, 2020; `luad_mskcc_2020`): missense in 106 patients, of which L858R accounts for 82.

Notes:
- Silent (synonymous) EGFR variants do not appear in any of these studies, as is typical for public cBioPortal data.
- The TCGA counts are per sample, and the MSK counts are per patient.
- These studies have no driver annotations in the database, so I have not labelled any variant as oncogenic or a driver.

Haiku 5.5 · ✓ Pass 31s · 320,118 tok · $0.012 · 5 LLM / 8 tool calls · transcript

**Short answer:** Crude outcomes differ. CIC-mutant tumors had fewer deaths, but that difference mostly tracks molecular subtype rather than CIC or ATRX status itself. I can't test significance here, so the survival comparison needs a Kaplan-Meier analysis.

**Study:** Brain Lower Grade Glioma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas), `lgg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018`, 514 patients. Counts are patients with overall survival data.

| Group (mutation status) | Patients | Deceased | Censored (alive at last follow-up) |
|---|---|---|---|
| ATRX mutant (CIC wild-type) | 189 | 46 (24%) | 143 |
| CIC mutant (ATRX wild-type) | 104 | 13 (13%) | 91 |
| Neither ATRX nor CIC mutant | 216 | 66 (31%) | 150 |
| Both ATRX and CIC mutant | 4 | 0 | 4 |

**Why the crude difference is mostly subtype:**
- Almost all CIC mutants (102 of 104) are IDH-mutant, 1p/19q-codeleted. Almost all ATRX mutants (183 of 189) are IDH-mutant, non-codeleted.
- Within the IDH-mutant codeleted subtype, CIC-mutant tumors died at about the same rate as the wild-type group (12/102 vs 9/62).
- The IDH-wildtype subtype has the highest death rate (46 of 85 wild-type patients). It contains only 7 ATRX mutants, so it doesn't inform the ATRX-vs-CIC comparison.
- Within IDH-mutant non-codeleted tumors, ATRX mutants had more deaths than wild-type (42/183 vs 9/62). Those wild-type numbers are small and mixed, so treat this as descriptive only.

**Survival medians:** In every group fewer than half of patients have died, so the Kaplan-Meier median is likely not reached in any of them. I haven't reported a median from raw follow-up times, because that would ignore censoring.

To see the Kaplan-Meier curves and log-rank test for the two groups:
[View ATRX mutant vs CIC mutant survival comparison](https://www.cbioportal.org/comparison/survival?comparisonId=6ab5b24fe497aad53593c238)

The comparison page shows 194 ATRX and 108 CIC patients, slightly more than above, because it doesn't require survival data to be present. To run a subtype-adjusted comparison, the Cox model would need to include subtype. That has to be done outside this database, in R or Python.
The answer correctly builds mutation-only groups (using the mutations molecular profile with just gene symbol queries, no CNA or fusion types included), links to a survival comparison page, and avoids inventing statistics like p-values or medians. All statistics reported are supported by the tool call results.

**Short answer:** In TCGA PanCancer Atlas data, the most distinctive co-altered genes are **APC, PIK3CA, and BRAF in KRAS-mutant colorectal cancer**, and **TP53 and EGFR (depleted) and STK11 and ATM (enriched) in KRAS-mutant lung adenocarcinoma**. These are mutation-only comparisons of KRAS-mutant vs. KRAS wild-type samples.

**Colorectal adenocarcinoma** ([Colorectal Adenocarcinoma, TCGA PanCancer Atlas](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=coadread_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018), 594 samples)

KRAS-mutant: 218 samples profiled; KRAS wild-type: 316 samples profiled.

| Gene | KRAS-mutant | KRAS wild-type | Difference (pts) |
|---|---|---|---|
| APC | 182/218 (83.5%) | 205/316 (64.9%) | +18.6 |
| PIK3CA | 83/218 (38.1%) | 64/316 (20.3%) | +17.8 |
| PCBP1 | 20/218 (9.2%) | 5/316 (1.6%) | +7.6 |
| TMPRSS15 | 19/218 (8.7%) | 7/316 (2.2%) | +6.5 |
| BRAF | 6/218 (2.8%) | 56/316 (17.7%) | −14.9 |
| COL7A1 | 7/218 (3.2%) | 40/316 (12.7%) | −9.5 |

BRAF and NRAS are depleted in KRAS-mutant tumors, which fits the general pattern that RAS and RAF alterations rarely co-occur. Several genes at the bottom of the list (ABCA13, DOCK3, ZNF469) are large, passenger-prone genes, so their differences are less informative.

- These are descriptive frequencies only. I didn't run a statistical test, so I can't say whether the differences are significant. The mutation counts also aren't filtered for driver status, because these studies don't carry driver annotations.
